View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

PolandBall is playing a game with EnemyBall. The rules are simple. Players have to say words in turns. You cannot say a word which was already said. PolandBall starts. The Ball which can't say a new word loses.

You're given two lists of words familiar to PolandBall and EnemyBall. Can you determine who wins the game, if both play optimally?


-----Input-----

The first input line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3) — number of words PolandBall and EnemyBall know, respectively.

Then n strings follow, one per line — words familiar to PolandBall.

Then m strings follow, one per line — words familiar to EnemyBall.

Note that one Ball cannot know a word more than once (strings are unique), but some words can be known by both players.

Each word is non-empty and consists of no more than 500 lowercase English alphabet letters.


-----Output-----

In a single line of print the answer — "YES" if PolandBall wins and "NO" otherwise. Both Balls play optimally.


-----Examples-----
Input
5 1
polandball

a
cool
character
nope

Output
YES
Input

kremowka
wadowicka
kremowka
wiedenska

Output
YES
Input

Output

-----Note-----

In the first example PolandBall knows much more words and wins effortlessly.

In the second example if PolandBall says kremowka first, then EnemyBall cannot use that word anymore. EnemyBall can only say wiedenska. PolandBall says wadowicka and wins.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. PolandBall and EnemyBall take turns saying words
2. PolandBall goes first
3. No word can be repeated (each player cannot say a word they've already said)
4. A player loses when they can't say a new word
5. We need to determine who wins with optimal play

## Key Insights

This is a classic game theory problem. Let me think about the strategy:

1. When a player says a word, they remove that word from both players' available word sets
2. If a word is known by both players, saying it by one player removes it from both players' available words
3. Players play optimally, meaning they will try to maximize their chances of winning

## Strategy Analysis

Let's consider different cases:

**Case 1: Only words known by one player**
- PolandBall can always say one of their exclusive words
- After PolandBall says an exclusive word, EnemyBall has fewer options

**Case 2: Words known by both players**
- If PolandBall says a word known by both, it removes that word from both players' available sets
- This reduces the total number of available words for both players

**Case 3: Optimal play**
- PolandBall should always prioritize saying words that are known only by them (since enemy can't use them)
- If PolandBall says a word known by both, it removes that word from both players' options

- Words known only by PolandBall (P_only)
- Words known only by EnemyBall (E_only)  
- Words known by both players (both)

- PolandBall will first say one of their exclusive words (P_only)
- Then EnemyBall will say one of their exclusive words (E_only)
- The game continues with remaining exclusive words and shared words

The critical point is that words known by both players are "shared" resources - if PolandBall takes one, it reduces the pool for both.

Let me recalculate the exclusive words:

- Exclusive to PolandBall: words only known to PolandBall
- Exclusive to EnemyBall: words only known to EnemyBall
- Shared: words known to both

In example 3:
- PolandBall: "a" (shared with EnemyBall) → p_exclusive = 0
- EnemyBall: "a" (shared), "b" (exclusive) → e_exclusive = 1

So p_exclusive = 0, e_exclusive = 1, shared = 1

Condition: (p_exclusive > e_exclusive) or (p_exclusive == e_exclusive and shared > 0)
0 > 1: false
0 == 1: false
false or false = false → NO ✓

In example 2:
- P: "kremowka" (shared), "wadowicka" (exclusive) → p_exclusive = 1
- E: "kremowka" (shared), "wiedenska" (exclusive) → e_exclusive = 1
- shared = 1

0 > 1: false
1 == 1 and shared > 0 → true → YES ✓

In example 1:
- P: "polandball", "is", "a", "cool", "character" (all exclusive)
- E: "nope" (exclusive)
- p_exclusive = 5, e_exclusive = 1
- 5 > 1 → true → YES ✓

Perfect!

The solution is: PolandBall wins if (p_exclusive > e_exclusive) or (p_exclusive == e_exclusive and shared > 0)

In other words, if PolandBall has strictly more exclusive words, or if they have the same number of exclusive words and at least one shared word.

```python
# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

poland_words = []
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.append(input().strip())

enemy_words = []
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.append(input().strip())

# Convert to sets
poland_set = set(poland_words)
enemy_set = set(enemy_words)

# Calculate exclusive and shared words
p_exclusive = len(poland_set - enemy_set)
e_exclusive = len(enemy_set - poland_set)
shared = len(poland_set & enemy_set)

# PolandBall wins if (p_exclusive > e_exclusive) or (p_exclusive == e_exclusive and shared > 0)
if p_exclusive > e_exclusive or (p_exclusive == e_exclusive and shared > 0):
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

## Final Solution

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

poland_words = []
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.append(input().strip())

enemy_words = []
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.append(input().strip())

# Convert to sets for fast lookup
poland_set = set(poland_words)
enemy_set = set(enemy_words)

# PolandBall wins if there's at least one word in PolandBall's list not in EnemyBall's list
if poland_set - enemy_set:  # non-empty set difference
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

poland_words = []
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.append(input().strip())

enemy_words = []
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.append(input().strip())

# Convert to sets for fast lookup
poland_set = set(poland_words)
enemy_set = set(enemy_words)

# PolandBall wins if there's at least one word in PolandBall's list not in EnemyBall's list
if poland_set - enemy_set:  # non-empty set difference
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. PolandBall and EnemyBall take turns saying words
2. PolandBall goes first
3. No word can be repeated (each player can only use words they know)
4. A player loses when they can't say a new word
5. We need to determine who wins with optimal play

## Key Insights

1. The game ends when one player has no available words to say
2. Both players play optimally, meaning they will try to maximize their own chances
3. Words that are known by both players create a critical situation - if one player uses such a word, the other player cannot use it anymore

## Strategy Analysis

Let me think about the optimal strategy:

- When a word is known by both players, the first player to use it effectively "blocks" that word for the opponent
- The player who can force the opponent into a position where they have no available words wins

Let's define:
- `poland_words`: words only known by PolandBall
- `enemy_words`: words only known by EnemyBall  
- `common_words`: words known by both players

The game proceeds as follows:
1. PolandBall starts first
2. If PolandBall has only common words, it will use one to block EnemyBall
3. After that, EnemyBall can only use its unique words
4. PolandBall can then use its unique words

## Optimal Play Strategy

With optimal play:
- PolandBall should use a common word if it exists (to block EnemyBall)
- After blocking, EnemyBall can only use its unique words
- PolandBall can then use its other words

The key insight is:
- If PolandBall has at least one common word, it can use it first to block EnemyBall
- After blocking, EnemyBall has only its unique words (enemy_words - common_words)
- PolandBall then has its unique words plus the remaining common words

So PolandBall wins if:
1. PolandBall has any unique words (poland_words), OR
2. PolandBall has common words and can block EnemyBall effectively

Actually, let me think more systematically:

After PolandBall uses a common word:
- EnemyBall cannot use that word anymore
- EnemyBall can only use its original unique words (enemy_words - common_words)
- PolandBall can use all its unique words + remaining common words

PolandBall wins if:
- PolandBall has any words available (either unique or common) that EnemyBall cannot use

More precisely: PolandBall wins if and only if after blocking (if possible), PolandBall has more words than EnemyBall.

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: 
- Poland: [polandball, is, a, cool, character]
- Enemy: [nope]
- Poland has many words, Enemy has only one. Poland wins.

Example 2:
- Poland: [kremowka, wadowicka]
- Enemy: [kremowka, wiedenska]
- Both know kremowka. Poland starts, uses kremowka → Enemy can only use wiedenska. Then Poland uses wadowicka → Poland wins.

Example 3:
- Poland: [a]
- Enemy: [a, b]
- Poland has only 'a', Enemy has 'a' and 'b'
- Poland uses 'a' → Enemy can only use 'b' → Poland has no more words → Enemy wins → Poland loses

So the pattern is:
1. PolandBall goes first
2. If there are common words, PolandBall should use one to block EnemyBall
3. After blocking, EnemyBall has only its unique words
4. PolandBall has all its unique words + remaining common words

PolandBall wins if after the block, PolandBall has more words available than EnemyBall has after the block.

Actually, since players alternate and PolandBall starts, PolandBall wins if PolandBall can make the last move.

The game ends when one player has no words left.

After PolandBall blocks a common word:
- EnemyBall can use only enemy_words - common_words
- PolandBall can use poland_words - 1 (after using one common word) + (common_words - 1)

But actually, after blocking, PolandBall can use all its remaining words.

The key is: PolandBall wins if (number of words PolandBall has after blocking) > (number of words EnemyBall has after blocking).

Wait, no. Since turns alternate and PolandBall starts, PolandBall wins if there are more moves available to PolandBall after the blocking.

Let me simplify:

Case 1: No common words
- PolandBall has poland_words words
- EnemyBall has enemy_words words
- PolandBall goes first, so PolandBall wins if poland_words > enemy_words (since PolandBall starts)

Case 2: There are common words
- PolandBall uses one common word first (obviously optimal)
- Now EnemyBall can only use the words in enemy_words that are not common (enemy_words - common_words)
- PolandBall can use all remaining words (poland_words - 1 + common_words - 1 = poland_words + common_words - 2)

Actually, after blocking:
- EnemyBall has (enemy_words - common_words) words left
- PolandBall has (poland_words + common_words - 2) words left (since it used 1 common word)

But PolandBall goes first in the remaining game.

The total moves available:
- After the initial block, there are (poland_words + common_words - 2) + (enemy_words - common_words) = poland_words + enemy_words - 2 moves

Since PolandBall goes first, PolandBall makes the 1st, 3rd, 5th, ... moves.

PolandBall wins if the total moves (after block) is odd, or if PolandBall makes the last move.

The number of moves after block is: (poland_words + common_words - 2) + (enemy_words - common_words) = poland_words + enemy_words - 2

PolandBall wins if this total is odd (since it goes first).

But let me verify with examples:

Example 1: No common words
- poland_words = 5, enemy_words = 1
- Moves after block: 5 + 1 - 2 = 4 (even) → EnemyBall wins? But answer is YES.

Wait, I'm making a mistake. After PolandBall uses one of its words, the game continues with EnemyBall's turn.

The key insight from known similar problems:

In this game, with optimal play:
- If PolandBall has at least one word that is not known by EnemyBall (i.e., only known by PolandBall), PolandBall wins
- If all words are common, then PolandBall can still win by using a common word to block, and then using its remaining words

Actually, I recall that in such games, the first player wins if the difference in available words (considering common words) is favorable.

After research and thinking about known problems, the solution is:

PolandBall wins if and only if:
- Either PolandBall has any unique words (not known by EnemyBall), OR
- PolandBall has a common word and can prevent EnemyBall from having enough words

But looking at the examples:

Example 2: Poland has kremowka, wadowicka; Enemy has kremowka, wiedenska
- Common word: kremowka
- Poland uses kremowka first → Enemy can only use wiedenska
- Poland uses wadowicka → Poland wins

Example 3: Poland has a; Enemy has a, b
- Poland uses a → Enemy uses b → Poland has no words → Enemy wins

I think the simpler rule is:
- If after PolandBall uses a common word, EnemyBall has no words left, PolandBall wins
- Otherwise, if EnemyBall can make a move after PolandBall's first move, then PolandBall wins if it can make more moves than EnemyBall

The winning condition is:
- PolandBall wins if (poland_words + common_words - 1) > (enemy_words)

Wait, let me think differently.

I found a known similar problem: 
- PolandBall wins if it has at least one word that is not shared with EnemyBall.

But example 2 has shared words and Poland wins, example 3 has shared words but Poland loses.

In example 3, Poland has only 'a' which is shared. After Poland uses 'a', Enemy can use 'b', so Enemy wins.

In example 2, after Poland uses 'kremowka', Enemy can only use 'wiedenska' (which is unique to Enemy), and then Poland uses 'wadowicka'.

So the key is whether PolandBall can make a move after EnemyBall's response.

The rule I believe is:
- PolandBall wins if the total number of words (after considering that common words are blocked) is such that PolandBall has the advantage.

After careful thought, I believe the solution is:

PolandBall wins if and only if:
- (poland_words + common_words) > enemy_words

Let me verify:
Example 1: 
- poland_words = 5, enemy_words = 1, common_words = 0
- (5 + 0) > 1 → 5 > 1 → YES ✓

Example 2:
- poland_words = 2, enemy_words = 2, common_words = 1  
- (2 + 1) > 2 → 3 > 2 → YES ✓

Example 3:
- poland_words = 1, enemy_words = 2, common_words = 1
- (1 + 1) > 2 → 2 > 2 → False → NO ✓

This matches all examples!

## Algorithm

1. Read n, m
2. Read n words for PolandBall
3. Read m words for EnemyBall
4. Find common words between the two lists
5. Calculate: (number of PolandBall words + number of common words) > (number of EnemyBall words)
6. Print "YES" if true, "NO" otherwise

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

poland_words = []
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.append(input().strip())

enemy_words = []
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.append(input().strip())

# Find common words
common_words = set(poland_words) & set(enemy_words)
common_count = len(common_words)

# PolandBall wins if (its words + common words) > enemy words
poland_total = n + common_count
enemy_total = m

if poland_total > enemy_total:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. PolandBall and EnemyBall take turns saying words
2. PolandBall goes first
3. No word can be repeated (each word can only be said once)
4. The player who cannot say a new word loses
5. We need to determine who wins with optimal play

## Key Insights

The game is about who can make the last move. Since both players play optimally, they will always try to maximize their chances of winning.

Let me think about the strategy:

1. If a word is known by both players, it's a "shared" word - whoever says it first prevents the opponent from using it.

2. The total number of unique words available is `len(set(PolandBall_words) ∪ EnemyBall_words)`.

3. However, the critical insight is about the shared words:
   - If a word is known by both players, the first player to use it eliminates that word for the other player
   - This gives PolandBall a strategic advantage

4. The game essentially becomes: how many words can PolandBall use before EnemyBall runs out of options?

## Strategy Analysis

Let's define:
- `only_Poland`: words only known to PolandBall
- `only_Enemy`: words only known to EnemyBall  
- `shared`: words known to both players

When PolandBall starts:
- PolandBall can choose any of the words (from his knowledge)
- After PolandBall uses a word, EnemyBall can respond with any of his words
- The game continues with alternating turns

With optimal play:
- PolandBall will use a shared word first to deprive EnemyBall of that word
- This way, EnemyBall loses a word immediately, reducing his options

The key insight is:
- If PolandBall can take a shared word on his first turn, then EnemyBall loses that word
- After that, the game reduces to a race between the remaining available words

Actually, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: PolandBall has 5 words, EnemyBall has 1 word. PolandBall wins.
- PolandBall has many more words, so he can always respond to EnemyBall's moves.

Example 2: PolandBall has kremowka, wadowicka; EnemyBall has kremowka, wiedenska
- PolandBall says "kremowka" (shared word) → EnemyBall cannot use "kremowka"
- EnemyBall says "wiedenska" (only word)
- PolandBall says "wadowicka" → EnemyBall has no more words → PolandBall wins

Example 3: PolandBall has "a", EnemyBall has "a", "b"
- PolandBall says "a" (shared word) → EnemyBall cannot use "a"
- EnemyBall says "b" → PolandBall has no more words → PolandBall loses

Wait, this suggests: PolandBall wins only if after removing shared words, he still has more words than EnemyBall.

Actually, let me think differently:

The game ends when one player cannot make a move. With optimal play:
- PolandBall will always choose a shared word if possible (to reduce EnemyBall's options)
- If there are no shared words, then the player who has more words wins (since PolandBall goes first)

But if both have available words after removing shared ones, it becomes a race:
- After PolandBall takes a shared word, EnemyBall can use his words (both unique and shared that were previously available)
- Actually, once a shared word is taken, it's gone from both players' pools

The key is: the shared words give PolandBall an advantage because he can eliminate an option from EnemyBall immediately.

Final insight:
- Let `s` = number of shared words
- Let `p` = words only in PolandBall
- Let `e` = words only in EnemyBall

With optimal play:
- PolandBall will use a shared word first (if available)
- This eliminates one of EnemyBall's potential moves
- After that, the game proceeds with remaining words

Actually, the winning condition is:
- PolandBall wins if either:
  1. He has more unique words than EnemyBall, OR
  2. He can use shared words to his advantage

But looking at the examples more carefully:

Example 3: PolandBall has 1 word ("a"), EnemyBall has 2 words ("a", "b")
- PolandBall takes "a" → EnemyBall has only "b"
- EnemyBall takes "b" → PolandBall has no moves → PolandBall loses → "NO"

The pattern seems to be: PolandBall wins if he has more unique words after the shared words are resolved, but actually let me think about the total turns.

Wait - I think the simpler rule is:
PolandBall wins if and only if the total number of words he can use (including shared) is greater than what EnemyBall can use after PolandBall takes a shared word.

Actually, I think the winner is determined by who has more total unique words, but with the turn order.

After analysis, here's the correct approach:

1. Count shared words
2. After PolandBall takes a shared word (if any), EnemyBall loses that word
3. The game continues with remaining words

The key insight: PolandBall wins if the total number of words available to him (all his words) is greater than the number of words available to EnemyBall after PolandBall takes a shared word.

But a simpler solution from known game theory:

PolandBall wins if the number of words he can use > number of words EnemyBall can use, considering turn order.

After reading the problem again, I believe the solution is:

PolandBall wins if the total number of words known only by PolandBall + the number of shared words is greater than the number of words known only by EnemyBall.

Wait, let me reconsider example 3:
- PolandBall has "a" (shared)
- EnemyBall has "a" (shared), "b" (unique)
- If PolandBall takes "a" first: EnemyBall has only "b", PolandBall has nothing left
- PolandBall loses

So the condition is: PolandBall wins if his unique words (plus the shared words he can use) > EnemyBall's unique words after shared words are gone.

Actually, the correct solution is:

PolandBall wins if the total number of words he knows (all words) is greater than the total number of words EnemyBall knows (all words), OR if he has the first move advantage.

But example 2 shows FranceBall wins when:
- PolandBall: kremowka, wadowicka (2 words)
- EnemyBall: kremowka, wiedenska (2 words)
- PolandBall wins

In this case, after shared word is taken, PolandBall has 1 unique word, EnemyBall has 1 word.

The pattern: PolandBall wins if (unique_Poland + shared) > (unique_Enemy)

Wait, let's check:
- Example 1: PolandBall: 5 words (all unique), EnemyBall: 1 word (unique)
  - unique_Poland + shared = 5 + 0 = 5
  - unique_Enemy = 1
  - 5 > 1 → YES ✓

- Example 2: PolandBall: 2 words, EnemyBall: 2 words
  - shared = 1 (kremowka)
  - unique_Poland = 1 (wadowicka)
  - unique_Enemy = 1 (wiedenska)
  - unique_Poland + shared = 1 + 1 = 2
  - unique_Enemy = 1
  - 2 > 1 → YES ✓

- Example 3: PolandBall: 1 word (a), EnemyBall: 2 words (a,b)
  - shared = 1 (a)
  - unique_Poland = 0
  - unique_Enemy = 1 (b)
  - unique_Poland + shared = 0 + 1 = 1
  - unique_Enemy = 1
  - 1 > 1 → False → NO ✓

Wait, this doesn't work because 1 > 1 is false, so NO which is correct.

But actually I think it's:
PolandBall wins if (unique_Poland + shared) > unique_Enemy

In example 3: (0 + 1) > 1 → 1 > 1 → False → NO ✓

Yes! This seems to work.

So the solution:
1. Find shared words between the two sets
2. unique_Poland = len(PolandBall_words - shared_words)
3. unique_Enemy = len(EnemyBall_words - shared_words)
4. shared = len(shared_words)
5. PolandBall wins if (unique_Poland + shared) > unique_Enemy

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

poland_words = set()
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.add(input().strip())

enemy_words = set()
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.add(input().strip())

# Find shared words
shared_words = poland_words.intersection(enemy_words)
unique_poland = len(poland_words - shared_words)
unique_enemy = len(enemy_words - shared_words)
shared = len(shared_words)

# PolandBall wins if (his unique words + shared words) > enemy's unique words
if unique_poland + shared > unique_enemy:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

poland_words = set()
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.add(input().strip())

enemy_words = set()
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.add(input().strip())

unique_poland = len(poland_words - shared_words)
unique_enemy = len(enemy_words - shared_words)
shared = len(shared_words)

# PolandBall wins if (his unique words + shared words) > enemy's unique words
if unique_poland + shared > unique_enemy:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. PolandBall and EnemyBall take turns saying words
2. PolandBall starts first
3. No word can be repeated (each word can only be said once)
4. Both players play optimally
5. The player who can't say a new word loses
6. We need to determine if PolandBall wins ("YES") or not ("NO")

## Key Insights

Since both players play optimally, they will try to maximize their chances of winning.

The critical insight is about the words that both players know:
- Words known by both players are "shared" words
- If PolandBall says a shared word first, EnemyBall can't use it anymore
- If EnemyBall says a shared word first, PolandBall can't use it anymore

So the strategy is:
1. Count words that only PolandBall knows
2. Count words that only EnemyBall knows  
3. Count words that both know (shared)

The key observation is that shared words are dangerous - they can be used to block the opponent.

## Strategy Analysis

Let me think about the optimal play:
- If PolandBall has more words than EnemyBall, PolandBall wins (since they start first)
- If there are shared words, the player who goes first (PolandBall) can use a shared word to eliminate EnemyBall's option

Actually, let's think more carefully:

1. PolandBall can use a shared word first to eliminate it from EnemyBall's pool
2. After that, EnemyBall can only use words that only they know
3. PolandBall can then use their remaining words (exclusive + shared if not used)

But if PolandBall doesn't use a shared word, EnemyBall might use one and block PolandBall.

Since both play optimally, PolandBall will use a shared word if it benefits them.

The winning condition depends on who has more "unique" words plus the ability to use shared words strategically.

After analyzing the examples:
- In example 1: PolandBall has 5 words, EnemyBall has 1. PolandBall wins.
- In example 2: Shared words are "kremowka". PolandBall says it first, EnemyBall can only say "wiedenska", then PolandBall says "wadowicka" and wins.
- In example 3: Both know "a", PolandBall has only "a" (unique), EnemyBall has "a" (shared) and "b" (unique). PolandBall says "a", then EnemyBall can say "b". PolandBall has no words left, so EnemyBall wins.

## Key Insight

PolandBall wins if and only if:
- PolandBall has more words than EnemyBall, OR
- The total number of words PolandBall knows (including shared) is greater than the total number of words EnemyBall knows.

Wait, let me reconsider with optimal play:

Actually, the optimal strategy:
- If there are shared words, PolandBall can use one to block EnemyBall
- The effective game becomes: PolandBall can use (unique_PolandBall + 1) words and EnemyBall can use (unique_EnemyBall) words

- P_only: words only PolandBall knows
- E_only: words only EnemyBall knows  
- Shared: words known by both

Total words for PolandBall: P_only + Shared
Total words for EnemyBall: E_only + Shared

Since PolandBall goes first, they can win if they have more total words than EnemyBall, OR if they can force EnemyBall into a losing position.

But both play optimally. The key is that shared words are special - PolandBall can use one to eliminate a potential move from EnemyBall.

Actually, the correct analysis is:
- PolandBall wins if the number of unique words PolandBall knows plus the number of shared words is strictly greater than the number of unique words EnemyBall knows plus the number of shared words.

This is because:
1. PolandBall starts first
2. For any shared word, PolandBall can use it to eliminate it from EnemyBall's options
3. After PolandBall uses one shared word, EnemyBall has (E_only) words left
4. PolandBall then has (P_only + Shared - 1) words left

The game continues with PolandBall having advantage due to starting first.

After more thought, the answer is simpler:

PolandBall wins if (P_only + Shared) > (E_only + Shared), which simplifies to P_only > E_only.

Wait, this doesn't match the examples.

Let me recheck example 3:
- P_only = 0 (PolandBall knows "a" which is shared)
- E_only = 1 (EnemyBall knows "b" which is unique)
- Shared = 1 ("a" is known by both)

PolandBall has 1 word (shared), EnemyBall has 2 words (shared + unique).
PolandBall goes first, says "a", EnemyBall says "b", PolandBall has nothing left. EnemyBall wins.

- EnemyBall: 1 unique + 1 shared = 2 words
- PolandBall wins

But let me verify: if PolandBall has equal or fewer words, they lose.

In example 3: PolandBall has 1 word, EnemyBall has 2 words → PolandBall loses → "NO" ✓

So the solution is: PolandBall wins if number of words PolandBall knows > number of words EnemyBall knows.

```python
# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

poland_words = []
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. PolandBall and EnemyBall take turns saying words
2. PolandBall goes first
3. No word can be repeated (can't say a word that was already said)
4. Players can only use words they know
5. The player who cannot say a new word loses
6. Both players play optimally

## Key Insight

This is a game theory problem. Since both players play optimally, we need to think about the strategy:

- Players will try to maximize their chances of winning
- When a word is used by one player, it becomes unavailable for both players
- The key is to understand which words are exclusive to each player vs. shared words

## Strategy Analysis

1. **Exclusive words**: Words known only by one player
   - If PolandBall has exclusive words, she can use them first
   - If EnemyBall has exclusive words, she can use them when PolandBall can't respond

2. **Shared words**: Words known by both players
   - These are "assets" that can be used by either player
   - The first player to use a shared word removes it from both players' access

3. **Game outcome**:
   - PolandBall starts first
   - If PolandBall can force a win regardless of EnemyBall's optimal play, she wins
   - Otherwise, EnemyBall wins

## Optimal Strategy

Since both play optimally:
- PolandBall will use an exclusive word first if available (to prevent EnemyBall from using shared words)
- If PolandBall has no exclusive words, she'll use a shared word
- EnemyBall will respond optimally

The critical insight is that shared words create a "count" that can be used by either player. Since PolandBall goes first, she has the advantage in using shared words.

The game essentially comes down to:
- If PolandBall has more exclusive words than EnemyBall, she wins
- If PolandBall has no exclusive words, the outcome depends on the count of shared words and exclusive words

Actually, let me reconsider with simpler logic:

Since PolandBall goes first, she wins if:
1. She has any exclusive words (she can use them first)
2. Or if she can force a win even without exclusive words

But the optimal strategy is:
- PolandBall will use a shared word if she has no exclusive words
- EnemyBall will respond with a shared word (if available)

The key is that the player who runs out of words first loses.

Let me model it properly:

- Let `P_exclusive` = words only PolandBall knows
- Let `E_exclusive` = words only EnemyBall knows  
- Let `shared` = words known by both

PolandBall's moves:

- Can use any of the shared words

EnemyBall's moves:
- Can use any of her exclusive words after PolandBall
- Can use any of the shared words after PolandBall

Since both play optimally, PolandBall will use her exclusive words if available (to prevent EnemyBall from using the same words).

After PolandBall uses an exclusive word or a shared word, EnemyBall can respond.

The game state is determined by the available words.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read PolandBall's words
poland_words = []
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.append(input().strip())

# Read EnemyBall's words
enemy_words = []
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.append(input().strip())

# PolandBall wins if she knows at least as many words as EnemyBall
if n >= m:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

PolandBall is playing a game with EnemyBall. The rules are simple. Players have to say words in turns. You cannot say a word which was already said. PolandBall starts. The Ball which can't say a new word loses.

You're given two lists of words familiar to PolandBall and EnemyBall. Can you determine who wins the game, if both play optimally?


-----Input-----

The first input line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3) — number of words PolandBall and EnemyBall know, respectively.

Then n strings follow, one per line — words familiar to PolandBall.

Then m strings follow, one per line — words familiar to EnemyBall.

Note that one Ball cannot know a word more than once (strings are unique), but some words can be known by both players.

Each word is non-empty and consists of no more than 500 lowercase English alphabet letters.


-----Output-----

In a single line of print the answer — "YES" if PolandBall wins and "NO" otherwise. Both Balls play optimally.


-----Examples-----

Output
YES
Input

kremowka
wadowicka
kremowka
wiedenska

Output
YES
Input
1 2
a
a
b

Output
NO

In the first example PolandBall knows much more words and wins effortlessly.

In the second example if PolandBall says kremowka first, then EnemyBall cannot use that word anymore. EnemyBall can only say wiedenska. PolandBall says wadowicka and wins.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Key Insights

Since both players play optimally, we need to think about the strategy:

1. **Shared words**: Words known by both players are "critical" - if one player uses a shared word, the other player loses the ability to use that word.

2. **Optimal strategy**: 
   - A player will try to use shared words to eliminate the opponent's options
   - After using a shared word, the opponent can only use words they know that aren't shared
   - Once a shared word is used, the remaining available words for each player are their unique words

3. **Winning condition**: 
   - If PolandBall has enough words (unique + shared) such that after optimally playing, she can always find a word when EnemyBall can't, she wins.
   - The game ends when one player runs out of valid words to say.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

poland_words = []
for _ in range(n):
    poland_words.append(input().strip())

enemy_words = []
for _ in range(m):
    enemy_words.append(input().strip())

# Convert to sets

set_enemy = set(enemy_words)

# Calculate unique and shared words
unique_poland = len(set_poland - set_enemy)
unique_enemy = len(set_enemy - set_poland)
shared = len(set_poland & set_enemy)

# Total words for each

total_enemy = unique_enemy + shared

# PolandBall wins if she has more words
if total_poland > total_enemy:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Polycarp loves geometric progressions very much. Since he was only three years old, he loves only the progressions of length three. He also has a favorite integer k and a sequence a, consisting of n integers.

He wants to know how many subsequences of length three can be selected from a, so that they form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A subsequence of length three is a combination of three such indexes i1, i2, i3, that 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < i3 ≤ n. That is, a subsequence of length three are such groups of three elements that are not necessarily consecutive in the sequence, but their indexes are strictly increasing.

A geometric progression with common ratio k is a sequence of numbers of the form b·k0, b·k1, ..., b·kr - 1.

Polycarp is only three years old, so he can not calculate this number himself. Help him to do it.

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers, n and k (1 ≤ n, k ≤ 2·105), showing how many numbers Polycarp's sequence has and his favorite number.

The second line contains n integers a1, a2, ..., an ( - 109 ≤ ai ≤ 109) — elements of the sequence.

Output

Output a single number — the number of ways to choose a subsequence of length three, such that it forms a geometric progression with a common ratio k.

Examples

Input

5 2
1 1 2 2 4


Output

4

Input

3 1
1 1 1


Output

1

Input

10 3
1 2 6 2 3 6 9 18 3 9


Output

6

Note

In the first sample test the answer is four, as any of the two 1s can be chosen as the first element, the second element can be any of the 2s, and the third element of the subsequence must be equal to 4.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b×k, b×k².

So we need to find triples (i, j, m) where i < j < m such that:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b×k  
- a[m] = b×k²

## Approach

For each possible middle element (at position j), I need to:
1. Count how many elements before position j have value = x
2. Count how many elements after position j have value = x×k² (where x is the value of the first element)

For each middle element with value v, I need to check when v = b×k, so b = v/k.

Then:
- Count of valid first elements = count of elements with value b = v/k (before position j)
- Count of valid third elements = count of elements with value b×k² = v×k (after position j)

I need to be careful about edge cases:
- k = 1: the progression is b, b, b (all elements equal)
- k > 1: we need to handle division properly
- k = 0: special case (only valid if b = 0, then progression is 0, 0, 0)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. First, handle the k = 1 case separately since it's simpler
2. For general case:
   - For each position j (as middle element), calculate what the first element should be (v/k) and what the third element should be (v×k²)
   - Count elements before j with value = v/k
   - Count elements after j with value = v×k²
   - Add the product of these counts to result

3. Use prefix and suffix arrays to efficiently count elements before and after each position.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, I can do this more efficiently:

For each middle element at position j with value `mid_val`:
- If k == 1: we need all triplet values to be the same
- If k != 1: first element should be `mid_val / k`, third element should be `mid_val * k * k`

But division needs to be exact. So I need to check if `mid_val` is divisible by k.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:
    # For k = 1, any three elements with the same value work
    # Count frequency of each value
    from collections import Counter
    count = Counter(a)
    result = 0
    for val, freq in count.items():
        if freq >= 3:
            # Choose 3 from freq elements
            result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
    print(result)
else:
    # k != 1
    result = 0
    
    # Create suffix count array: suffix[i][val] = count of val from position i to n-1
    suffix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Fill suffix counts from right to left
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        suffix_count[i] = suffix_count[i + 1].copy() if i + 1 < n else {}
        suffix_count[i][a[i]] = suffix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each position j as middle element
    for j in range(1, n - 1):  # middle element, can't be first or last
        mid_val = a[j]
        
        # What should be the first element? mid_val / k
        # What should be the third element? mid_val * k * k
        
        # Check if mid_val is divisible by k
        if mid_val % k == 0:
            first_val = mid_val // k
            third_val = mid_val * k * k
            
            # Count elements before j with value first_val
            first_count = 0
            for i in range(j):
                if a[i] == first_val:
                    first_count += 1
            
            # Count elements after j with value third_val
            third_count = suffix_count[j + 1].get(third_val, 0)
            
            result += first_count * third_count
    
    print(result)
```

Wait, I'm still not handling edge cases properly. Let me re-read the problem and think more carefully.

Actually, let me implement a cleaner solution:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:
    # For k = 1, we want three equal elements
    from collections import Counter
    count = Counter(a)
    result = 0
    for val, freq in count.items():
        if freq >= 3:
            result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
    print(result)
else:
    result = 0
    
    # Pre-compute suffix counts from right to left
    suffix = {}
    suffix[a[-1]] = 1
    
    # Process from right to left, using each position as middle element
    # Middle element at position j (0-indexed)
    for j in range(n-2, 0, -1):
        # Add current element to suffix count
        suffix[a[j]] = suffix.get(a[j], 0) + 1
        
        mid_val = a[j]
        
        # To form geometric progression: x, x*k, x*k^2
        # Since middle element is x*k, we have x = mid_val / k
        # And third element should be x*k^2 = mid_val * k
        
        if mid_val % k == 0:
            first_val = mid_val // k
            third_val = mid_val * k
            
            # Count elements before position j with value first_val
            first_count = 0
            for i in range(j):
                if a[i] == first_val:
                    first_count += 1
            
            # Count elements after position j with value third_val  
            third_count = suffix.get(third_val, 0)
            
            result += first_count * third_count
    
    print(result)
```

Wait, I made a mistake in the geometric progression. Let me correct:

In a geometric progression with ratio k: b, b*k, b*k²

If middle element is b*k, then:
- First element: b = (b*k) / k = middle_val / k  
- Third element: b*k² = (middle_val / k) * k² = middle_val * k

Yes, that's correct.

But let me verify with the first example:
n=5, k=2, a=[1,1,2,2,4]

Middle elements at positions 1,2,3,4 (0-indexed):
- j=1: a[1]=1, 1%2 != 0, skip
- j=2: a[2]=2, 2%2==0, first_val=1, third_val=4
  - Before j=2: positions 0,1 have values [1,1] → first_count=2
  - After j=2: positions 3,4 have values [2,4] → third_count=1 (only 4)
  - Add 2*1 = 2
- j=3: a[3]=2, 2%2==0, first_val=1, third_val=4  
  - Before j=3: positions 0,1,2 have values [1,1,2] → first_count=2
  - After j=3: position 4 has value 4 → third_count=1
  - Add 2*1 = 2
- j=4: can't be middle as we need j < n-1

Total = 4. This matches!

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:

    from collections import Counter
    count = Counter(a)

    suffix = {}

    if n > 1:
        suffix[a[-1]] = 1

    for j in range(n-2, 0, -1):
        # Add current element to suffix count
        suffix[a[j]] = suffix.get(a[j], 0) + 1

                    first_count += 1
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with common ratio k has the form: b, b*k, b*k².

So we need to find triplets (i, j, l) where i < j < l such that:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b*k  
- a[l] = b*k²

## Approach

1. For each possible middle element (at position j), we need to count:
   - How many elements before j have value = a[j]/k (which would be the first element)
   - How many elements after j have value = a[j]*k (which would be the third element)

2. The total count for position j would be: (count of a[j]/k before j) × (count of a[j]*k after j)

3. We need to be careful about the cases:
   - When k = 1: all elements must be equal
   - When k ≠ 1: we need to handle division properly

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle the special case k = 1**: All three elements must be equal, so we count the number of ways to choose 3 identical elements from each group of identical values.

2. **For k ≠ 1**: For each position j, count how many valid first and third elements exist.

3. **Key insight**: For a middle element a[j], we need:
   - First element value = a[j] / k (must be an integer)
   - Third element value = a[j] * k

4. **Efficient counting**: We can precompute frequency counts and use prefix/suffix arrays to quickly get the counts.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if k == 1:
        # When k = 1, we need three equal elements
        # Count frequency of each value
        freq = {}
        for val in a:
            freq[val] = freq.get(val, 0) + 1
        
        total = 0
        for count in freq.values():
            if count >= 3:
                total += count * (count - 1) * (count - 2) // 6
        return total
    
    # For k != 1
    total = 0
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - how many times each value appears from position i to end
    suffix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Fill suffix_count from right to left
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        suffix_count[i] = suffix_count[i + 1].copy() if i + 1 < n else {}
        suffix_count[i][a[i]] = suffix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each possible middle position
    for j in range(1, n - 1):
        middle_val = a[j]
        
        # Check if middle_val / k is an integer
        if middle_val % k != 0:
            continue
            
        first_val = middle_val // k
        third_val = middle_val * k
        
        # Count how many first values exist before position j
        first_count_before = 0
        for i in range(j):
            if a[i] == first_val:
                first_count_before += 1
        
        # Count how many third values exist after position j
        third_count_after = suffix_count[j + 1].get(third_val, 0)
        
        total += first_count_before * third_count_after
    
    return total

print(solve())
```

Wait, I can optimize this further. The above solution is O(n²) in the worst case. Let me optimize it:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:
    # When k = 1, we need three equal elements
    from collections import Counter
    freq = Counter(a)
    total = 0
    for count in freq.values():
        if count >= 3:
            total += count * (count - 1) * (count - 2) // 6
    print(total)
else:
    # For k != 1
    total = 0
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - how many times each value appears from position i to end
    suffix_count = {}
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        current_val = a[i]
        suffix_count[current_val] = suffix_count.get(current_val, 0) + 1
    
    # For each possible middle position
    prefix_count = {}
    for j in range(n):
        middle_val = a[j]
        
        # Check if middle_val / k is an integer
        if middle_val % k != 0:
            # No valid first element
            pass
        else:
            first_val = middle_val // k
            third_val = middle_val * k
            
            # Count third values after position j
            third_count_after = suffix_count.get(third_val, 0)
            
            # Count first values before position j
            first_count_before = prefix_count.get(first_val, 0)
            
            total += first_count_before * third_count_after
        
        # Add current element to prefix count for next iterations
        prefix_count[middle_val] = prefix_count.get(middle_val, 0) + 1
        
        # Remove current element from suffix count (for next iteration)
        suffix_count[middle_val] = suffix_count.get(middle_val, 0) - 1
        if suffix_count[middle_val] == 0:
            del suffix_count[middle_val]
    
    print(total)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b*k, b*k²

So we need to find triples (i, j, m) where i < j < m such that:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b*k  
- a[m] = b*k²

## Approach

1. For each possible middle element (at index j), we need to count:
   - How many elements before j equal to a[j]/k
   - How many elements after j equal to a[j]*k

2. Then multiply these two counts to get the number of valid triplets with j as the middle element.

3. Sum this over all possible middle positions.

## Special Cases to Consider

- When k = 1: The progression is b, b, b, so we need three identical elements.
- When k = 0: The progression is b, 0, 0, so we need b, then 0, then 0.
- When dealing with division: a[j]/k might not be an integer, so we need to check if it's valid.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle k = 1 separately**: When k = 1, we need to count the number of ways to choose 3 positions with the same value.

2. **For k ≠ 1**: For each position j, count how many elements before j equal to a[j]/k, and how many elements after j equal to a[j]*k.

3. **Validation**: Only consider valid geometric progressions - if a[j]/k is not an integer, skip.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

count = 0

# Handle the case when k = 1 separately
if k == 1:
    # We need to count triplets of same values
    freq = {}
    for val in a:
        freq[val] = freq.get(val, 0) + 1
    
    # For each value that appears freq times, number of triplets is C(freq, 3) = freq * (freq-1) * (freq-2) // 6
    for val in freq:
        freq_val = freq[val]
        if freq_val >= 3:
            count += freq_val * (freq_val - 1) * (freq_val - 2) // 6
else:
    # For k != 1, we can use the three-pointer approach
    # Precompute frequency of elements before each position
    before = {}
    
    # Process each position as the middle element
    for j in range(1, n - 1):  # j from 1 to n-2 (0-indexed)
        middle_val = a[j]
        
        # Check if we can form a valid geometric progression with middle value
        # First element should be middle_val / k
        first_val = middle_val / k
        
        # Check if first_val is an integer and is valid
        if first_val != int(first_val):
            continue
            
        first_val = int(first_val)
        
        # Second element should be middle_val * k
        second_val = middle_val * k
        
        # Count valid first elements (before position j)
        first_count = before.get(first_val, 0)
        
        # Count valid second elements (after position j)
        # We need to count elements after j with value = second_val
        # We'll compute this on the fly during iteration
        after_count = 0
        
        # We need to count after elements, so we'll do this differently
        # Let's restructure: we'll need to know the count of elements after j with value = second_val
        # We can precompute this or compute it incrementally
```

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

count = 0

if k == 1:
    # Count triplets of identical elements
    from collections import Counter
    freq = Counter(a)
    for val, cnt in freq.items():
        if cnt >= 3:
            count += cnt * (cnt - 1) * (cnt - 2) // 6
else:
    # For k != 1
    # Precompute the frequency of elements to the right of each position
    right_count = {}
    # Populate right_count from right to left
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        right_count[a[i]] = right_count.get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # Process each position as the middle element
    left_count = {}
    for j in range(1, n - 1):  # j from 1 to n-2 (0-indexed)
        middle_val = a[j]
        
        # First term should be middle_val / k
        if middle_val % k != 0:
            # Can't divide evenly
            pass
        else:
            first_val = middle_val // k
            
            # Count of first_val in positions [0, j-1]
            first_count = left_count.get(first_val, 0)
            
            # Count of second_val in positions [j+1, n-1]
            second_val = middle_val * k
            second_count = right_count.get(second_val, 0)
            
            count += first_count * second_count
        
        # Add current element to left_count
        left_count[a[j]] = left_count.get(a[j], 0) + 1
    
    # Update right_count after processing position j
    # Actually, we already have right_count from the initial loop
    # We need to remove a[j] from right_count after processing it
    # But we don't need to because we're only using right_count for positions > j
    # and we're processing j from left to right
```

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

count = 0

if k == 1:
    # Count triplets of identical elements
    from collections import Counter
    freq = Counter(a)
    for val, cnt in freq.items():
        if cnt >= 3:
            count += cnt * (cnt - 1) * (cnt - 2) // 6
else:
    # For k != 1
    # Precompute initial right counts
    right_count = {}
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        right_count[a[i]] = right_count.get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # Process each position as the middle element
    left_count = {}
    for j in range(1, n - 1):  # j from 1 to n-2 (0-indexed)
        middle_val = a[j]
        
        # First term should be middle_val / k
        if middle_val % k != 0:
            # Can't form geometric progression
            pass
        else:
            first_val = middle_val // k
            second_val = middle_val * k
            
            # Count of first_val in positions [0, j-1]
            first_count = left_count.get(first_val, 0)
            
            # Count of second_val in positions [j+1, n-1]
            second_count = right_count.get(second_val, 0)
            
            count += first_count * second_count
        
        # Add current element to left_count (for future positions)
        left_count[a[j]] = left_count.get(a[j], 0) + 1
        
        # Remove current element from right_count (since it's now processed)
        # We only need to remove it for positions > j
        right_count[a[j]] = right_count.get(a[j], 0) - 1
        if right_count[a[j]] == 0:
            del right_count[a[j]]

print(count)
```

A geometric progression with common ratio k is a sequence of numbers of the form b·k0, b·k1, ..., b·kr - 1.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b*k, b*k²

For a subsequence at positions i < j < k to form such a progression:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b*k 
- a[k] = b*k²

## Approach

For each middle element at position j (which would be the second element of the progression), I need to:
1. Count how many elements before position j have value equal to x
2. Count how many elements after position j have value equal to x*k
3. The contribution to the answer is (count_before) × (count_after)

The middle element must be b*k, so for each element a[j] that equals b*k, the first element should be b and the third element should be b*k².

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle the case when k = 1**: All elements are the same, so we need to count triplets from the same value.

2. **For k ≠ 1**: For each position j, if a[j] is some value, we check if it can be the middle element of a GP with ratio k.

3. **For each possible middle element value v**, we need to:
   - Count elements before j with value v/k
   - Count elements after j with value v*k

4. **Special case**: When k = 1, all elements are the same, so we need to count combinations of 3 elements from the same value.

5. **Optimization**: Instead of checking each position independently, I'll:
   - Precompute prefix counts (elements before each position)
   - Precompute suffix counts (elements after each position)

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if k == 1:
        # When k = 1, all elements in the progression are the same
        # We need to count combinations of 3 elements from each group of identical values
        count = {}
        for val in a:
            count[val] = count.get(val, 0) + 1
        
        result = 0
        for val, freq in count.items():
            if freq >= 3:
                result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
        
        print(result)
        return
    
    # For k != 1
    result = 0
    
    # Precompute prefix counts - for each position, count how many elements with value x are before it
    prefix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Initialize first position
    prefix_count[0] = {}
    if a[0] != 0:
        prefix_count[0][a[0]] = 1
    
    # Fill prefix counts
    for i in range(1, n):
        prefix_count[i] = prefix_count[i-1].copy()
        prefix_count[i][a[i]] = prefix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - for each position, count how many elements with value x are after it
    suffix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Initialize last position
    suffix_count[n-1] = {}
    if a[n-1] != 0:
        suffix_count[n-1][a[n-1]] = 1
    
    # Fill suffix counts
    for i in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        suffix_count[i] = suffix_count[i+1].copy()
        suffix_count[i][a[i]] = suffix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each position j, if a[j] can be the middle element of GP with ratio k
    for j in range(1, n-1):
        middle = a[j]
        
        # Check if middle can be b*k, so first element should be b and third should be b*k^2
        # So b = middle / k, and b*k^2 = middle * k
        
        if middle % k == 0:
            b = middle // k
            third_value = middle * k
            
            # Add contribution from all valid combinations
            # Count of elements with value b before position j
            count_before_b = prefix_count[j-1].get(b, 0)
            # Count of elements with value third_value after position j
            count_after_third = suffix_count[j+1].get(third_value, 0)
            
            result += count_before_b * count_after_third
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

```python
    print(result)

    prefix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Initialize first position
    prefix_count[0] = {}
    if a[0] != 0:

        prefix_count[i][a[i]] = prefix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - for each position, count how many elements with value x are after it
    suffix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Initialize last position
    suffix_count[n-1] = {}
    if a[n-1] != 0:
        suffix_count[n-1][a[n-1]] = 1
    
    # Fill suffix counts
    for i in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        suffix_count[i] = suffix_count[i+1].copy()
        suffix_count[i][a[i]] = suffix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each position j, if a[j] can be the middle element of GP with ratio k
    for j in range(1, n-1):
        middle = a[j]
        
        # Check if middle can be b*k, so first element should be b and third should be b*k^2
        # So b = middle / k, and b*k^2 = middle * k
        
        # Special case: if middle is 0, then b*k = 0, so b = 0, and third = 0
        if middle == 0:
            # b = 0, third = 0
            count_before_b = prefix_count[j-1].get(0, 0)
            count_after_third = suffix_count[j+1].get(0, 0)
            result += count_before_b * count_after_third
        else:
            if middle % k == 0:
                b = middle // k
                third_value = middle * k
                
                # Count of elements with value b before position j
                count_before_b = prefix_count[j-1].get(b, 0)
                # Count of elements with value third_value after position j
                count_after_third = suffix_count[j+1].get(third_value, 0)
                
                result += count_before_b * count_after_third
    
    print(result)
```

n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:
    # When k = 1, all elements are the same
    count = {}
    for val in a:
        count[val] = count.get(val, 0) + 1
    
    result = 0
    for val, freq in count.items():
        if freq >= 3:
            result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
    
    print(result)
else:
    result = 0
    
    # Precompute prefix counts - for each position, count how many elements with value x are before it
    prefix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Initialize first position
    prefix_count[0] = {}
    if a[0] != 0:
        prefix_count[0][a[0]] = 1
    
    # Fill prefix counts
    for i in range(1, n):
        prefix_count[i] = prefix_count[i-1].copy()
        prefix_count[i][a[i]] = prefix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - for each position, count how many elements with value x are after it
    suffix_count = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Initialize last position
    suffix_count[n-1] = {}
    if a[n-1] != 0:
        suffix_count[n-1][a[n-1]] = 1
    
    # Fill suffix counts
    for i in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        suffix_count[i] = suffix_count[i+1].copy()
        suffix_count[i][a[i]] = suffix_count[i].get(a[i], 0) + 1
    
    # For each position j, if a[j] can be the middle element of GP with ratio k
    for j in range(1, n-1):
        middle = a[j]
        
        # Check if middle can be b*k, so first element should be b and third should be b*k^2
        # So b = middle / k, and b*k^2 = middle * k
        
        # Special case: if middle is 0, then b*k = 0, so b = 0, and third = 0
        if middle == 0:
            # b = 0, third = 0
            count_before_b = prefix_count[j-1].get(0, 0)
            count_after_third = suffix_count[j+1].get(0, 0)
            result += count_before_b * count_after_third
        else:
            if middle % k == 0:
                b = middle // k
                third_value = middle * k
                
                # Count of elements with value b before position j
                count_before_b = prefix_count[j-1].get(b, 0)
                # Count of elements with value third_value after position j
                count_after_third = suffix_count[j+1].get(third_value, 0)
                
                result += count_before_b * count_after_third
    
    print(result)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Polycarp loves geometric progressions very much. Since he was only three years old, he loves only the progressions of length three. He also has a favorite integer k and a sequence a, consisting of n integers.

He wants to know how many subsequences of length three can be selected from a, so that they form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A subsequence of length three is a combination of three such indexes i1, i2, i3, that 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < i3 ≤ n. That is, a subsequence of length three are such groups of three elements that are not necessarily consecutive in the sequence, but their indexes are strictly increasing.

A geometric progression with common ratio k is a sequence of numbers of the form b·k0, b·k1, ..., b·kr - 1.

Polycarp is only three years old, so he can not calculate this number himself. Help him to do it.

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers, n and k (1 ≤ n, k ≤ 2·105), showing how many numbers Polycarp's sequence has and his favorite number.

The second line contains n integers a1, a2, ..., an ( - 109 ≤ ai ≤ 109) — elements of the sequence.

Output

Output a single number — the number of ways to choose a subsequence of length three, such that it forms a geometric progression with a common ratio k.

Examples

Input

5 2
1 1 2 2 4


Output

4

Input

3 1
1 1 1


Output

1

Input

10 3
1 2 6 2 3 6 9 18 3 9


Output

6

Note

In the first sample test the answer is four, as any of the two 1s can be chosen as the first element, the second element can be any of the 2s, and the third element of the subsequence must be equal to 4.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b*k, b*k²

So for three elements at indices i < j < k, we need:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b*k  
- a[k] = b*k²

This means: a[j] / a[i] = k and a[k] / a[j] = k

## Key Observations

1. For each middle element a[j], we need to count how many elements before it (i) satisfy a[i] = a[j]/k, and how many elements after it (k) satisfy a[k] = a[j]*k.

2. We need to be careful about division by zero and negative numbers.

3. We can use a frequency-based approach: for each position, we can precompute how many valid pairs exist.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Handle special cases**: When k = 1, the progression is constant (a, a, a)
2. **For each middle element**: Count valid first elements (before) and valid third elements (after)
3. **Sum up all combinations**

## Implementation Strategy

For each position j (from 1 to n-2, 0-indexed):
- Count how many elements before j have value a[j]/k
- Count how many elements after j have value a[j]*k
- Add the product of these counts to answer

We need to handle:
- Division by zero (k = 0)
- Negative numbers (division might not yield integer)
- Integer constraints

## Edge Cases

- k = 0: only possible if we have a, 0, 0 pattern
- k = 1: all elements equal form valid progression

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 0:
    # For k=0, we need pattern: b, 0, 0
    # First element can be any value, second must be 0, third must be 0
    count_0 = a.count(0)
    if count_0 >= 2:
        # We need to count valid positions
        result = 0
        # For each position j where a[j] = 0 and j < n-1
        for j in range(n-1):
            if a[j] == 0:
                # Count how many zeros after position j
                zeros_after = sum(1 for i in range(j+1, n) if a[i] == 0)
                result += zeros_after
        print(result)
    else:
        print(0)
else:
    result = 0
    
    # Precompute frequency counts for elements after each position
    # We'll go through each middle position
    after_count = [0] * n
    
    # First, fill after_count array
    # Count how many times each value appears after each position
    for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
        after_count[i] = {}
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            val = a[j]
            after_count[i][val] = after_count[i].get(val, 0) + 1
    
    # Now go through each middle position
    for j in range(1, n-1):
        mid_val = a[j]
        
        # Check if we can have a valid first element before j
        # First element should be mid_val / k
        first_target = mid_val / k
        
        # Check if first_target is integer
        if first_target != int(first_target):
            # Not divisible, skip
            continue
            
        first_target_int = int(first_target)
        
        # Check if third element should be mid_val * k
        third_target = mid_val * k
        
        # Count valid first elements before j
        first_count = 0
        for i in range(j):
            if a[i] == first_target_int:
                first_count += 1
        
        # Count valid third elements after j
        third_count = after_count[j].get(third_target, 0)
        
        result += first_count * third_count
    
    print(result)
```

Wait, this approach has issues with floating point and might be too slow. Let me optimize it:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:
    # For k=1, we need three equal elements
    # Count frequency of each value
    from collections import Counter
    count = Counter(a)
    result = 0
    for val, freq in count.items():
        if freq >= 3:
            # Number of ways to choose 3 from freq
            result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
    print(result)
else:
    result = 0
    
    # Precompute frequency after each position
    # After[i] will be a dictionary of value -> count for positions i+1 to n-1
    after = [{} for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Fill after array from right to left
    for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
        after[i] = after[i+1].copy() if i+1 < n else {}
        if i+1 < n:
            val = a[i+1]
            after[i][val] = after[i].get(val, 0) + 1
    
    # For each middle position
    for j in range(1, n-1):
        mid_val = a[j]
        
        # First element should be mid_val / k
        # Check if mid_val is divisible by k
        if mid_val % k != 0:
            continue
            
        first_val = mid_val // k
        
        # Third element should be mid_val * k
        third_val = mid_val * k
        
        # Count first elements before j
        first_count = sum(1 for i in range(j) if a[i] == first_val)
        
        # Count third elements after j
        third_count = after[j].get(third_val, 0)
        
        result += first_count * third_count
    
    print(result)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b×k, b×k².

Given a sequence a, we need to find triplets (i, j, l) where i < j < l such that:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b × k  
- a[l] = b × k²

## Approach

1. For each possible middle element (at position j), we need to count:
   - How many elements before j have value = a[j] / k
   - How many elements after j have value = a[j] × k

2. The total count for this middle element is: (count of valid first elements) × (count of valid third elements)

3. We need to be careful about edge cases:
   - When k = 1: all elements are equal, so we just need to count valid triplets
   - When k ≠ 1: we need to handle division and ensure values are integers

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle k = 1 case**: When k = 1, we need to count triplets where all three elements are equal. This is a combination problem: for each unique value, if it appears freq times, we can choose 3 elements in C(freq,3) = freq×(freq-1)×(freq-2)/6 ways.

2. **Handle k ≠ 1 case**: For each position j, count valid pairs:
   - Count of elements before j with value = a[j] / k (must be integer)
   - Count of elements after j with value = a[j] × k
   - Multiply these counts

3. **Optimization**: Instead of checking each position, we can precompute:
   - Prefix counts (how many times each value appears before each position)
   - Suffix counts (how many times each value appears after each position)

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if k == 1:
        # For k = 1, all elements in progression are equal
        # We need to count triplets of same value
        from collections import Counter
        count = Counter(a)
        result = 0
        for freq in count.values():
            if freq >= 3:
                result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
        return result
    
    # For k != 1
    result = 0
    
    # Precompute suffix counts - how many times each value appears after position i
    suffix_count = {}
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        # Add current element to suffix count
        val = a[i]
        suffix_count[val] = suffix_count.get(val, 0) + 1
        
        # Check if we can form progression with current element as middle
        # Need first element = val / k, third element = val * k
        # val / k must be integer
        if val % k == 0:
            first_val = val // k
            third_val = val * k
            
            # Count how many times first_val appeared before current position
            # and how many times third_val appears after current position
            prefix_count = 0
            # We need to maintain prefix counts as we go from left to right
            # So we need to restructure
            
    # Let's restart with correct approach
    # We'll go through each position as middle element
    prefix_count = {}
    suffix_count = {}
    
    # Initialize suffix counts with all elements
    for val in a:
        suffix_count[val] = suffix_count.get(val, 0) + 1
    
    result = 0
    
    for j in range(n):
        # Remove current element from suffix counts
        val_j = a[j]
        suffix_count[val_j] -= 1
        if suffix_count[val_j] == 0:
            del suffix_count[val_j]
        
        # For current element as middle, we want:
        # first element = val_j / k, third element = val_j * k
        if k == 1:
            # Already handled above
            pass
        else:
            # Check if val_j / k is integer
            if val_j % k == 0:
                first_val = val_j // k
                third_val = val_j * k
                
                # Count how many times first_val appeared before position j
                count_first = prefix_count.get(first_val, 0)
                # Count how many times third_val appears after position j
                count_third = suffix_count.get(third_val, 0)
                
                result += count_first * count_third
        
        # Add current element to prefix counts
        prefix_count[val_j] = prefix_count.get(val_j, 0) + 1
    
    return result

print(solve())
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of subsequences of length 3 that form a geometric progression with common ratio k.

A geometric progression with ratio k has the form: b, b×k, b×k²

So for three elements at positions i < j < k to form a GP with ratio k, we need:
- a[i] = b
- a[j] = b×k 
- a[k] = b×k²

This means: a[j] = a[i] × k and a[k] = a[j] × k = a[i] × k²

## Approach

1. For each possible middle element (at position j), we need to count:
   - How many elements before j have value equal to a[j] / k
   - How many elements after j have value equal to a[j] × k

2. The total count for middle element at position j would be: (count of valid first elements before j) × (count of valid third elements after j)

3. We need to handle the case when k = 1 separately since division by k would be problematic.

## Special Cases

- If k = 1: all three elements must be equal, so we need to count triples of identical elements
- If k = 0: only possible if first element is non-zero, second is 0, third is 0
- We need to handle division carefully with negative numbers and zero

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Preprocess**: Count frequency of each value in the array
2. **For each position j as middle element**:
   - Calculate expected first element: a[j] / k
   - Calculate expected third element: a[j] × k
   - Count valid first elements before position j
   - Count valid third elements after position j
   - Add to result

3. **Optimization**: Instead of scanning the entire array for each position, we can use prefix and suffix frequency arrays.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if k == 1:
    # All three elements must be equal
    from collections import Counter
    count = Counter(a)
    result = 0
    for val, freq in count.items():
        if freq >= 3:
            result += freq * (freq - 1) * (freq - 2) // 6
    print(result)
else:
    # k != 1
    result = 0
    
    # Create prefix and suffix frequency arrays
    # prefix[i][val] = count of val in positions 0 to i-1
    # suffix[i][val] = count of val in positions i+1 to n-1
    
    # First, create frequency maps for prefix and suffix
    prefix_freq = {}
    suffix_freq = {}
    
    # Initialize suffix frequencies (all elements initially)
    for val in a:
        suffix_freq[val] = suffix_freq.get(val, 0) + 1
    
    # Process each position as the middle element
    for j in range(n):
        # Current element at position j
        current = a[j]
        
        # What should be the first element? current / k
        first_val = current / k
        
        # What should be the third element? current * k
        third_val = current * k
        
        # Check if first_val is valid (integer)
        if first_val != int(first_val):
            # If not integer, skip this middle element
            pass
        else:
            first_val = int(first_val)
            
            # Check if third_val is valid (integer) - it always is for integer k and integer a[j]
            # But we need to ensure the division was exact
            
            # Count first elements before position j
            prefix_count = prefix_freq.get(first_val, 0)
            
            # Count third elements after position j
            suffix_count = suffix_freq.get(third_val, 0)
            
            result += prefix_count * suffix_count
        
        # Update prefix frequency for position j
        prefix_freq[current] = prefix_freq.get(current, 0) + 1
        
        # Remove current from suffix frequency
        suffix_freq[current] = suffix_freq.get(current, 0) - 1
        if suffix_freq[current] == 0:
            del suffix_freq[current]
    
    print(result)
```

Wait, there's an issue with floating point division. Let me fix this to avoid precision issues:
